NOTE TO CONSULTING ENGINEERS\na) The Occupational Health and Safety Act, Act 85 of 1993 and its Regulations together with SANS Codes\nset out minimum standards with regards to Occupational Health and Safety. The South African National\nRoads Agency SOC Limited (SANRAL), has developed this Occupational Health and Safety\nSpecifications with these minimum standards in mind and in certain stages the requirements of SANRAL\nexceeds the minimum legal requirements to follow best practices and to ensure a healthy and safe\nworkplace for all.\nb) SANRAL in no way assumes the Service Provider’s legal liabilities and responsibilities. The Service\nProvider is and remains accountable for the quality and execution of his health and safety programme\nfor his employees. This Health and Safety Specification reflects minimum legal and SANRAL require-\nments and should not be construed as all encompassing.\nc) It is very important for the Service Provider to note that when the Service Provider carry out any type of\nconstruction work, as per the definition, the Service Provider will be regarded as a Contractor, as per\nthe definition and must then comply with the requirements of the Construction Regulations and in\nparticular Section 7.\nd) It is realized that the Service Provider may have its own Health and Safety Management system and\nsafe work practices. The intention of this Health and Safety Specification is not to change the Service\nProvider’s Health and Safety management system, but for the Service Provider to use its current Health\nand Safety management system to draw up a project specific Health and Safety plan according to these\nspecifications as well as to legally comply with the Construction Regulations, GNR.84 of 2014.\ne) It is the responsibility of the Service Provider to make themselves conversant and comply with the\nrequirements and conditions contained in the various legislation pertaining to their profession and scope\nof works at all times.\nf) This document is not exhaustive of all duties imposed by the Occupational Health and Safety Act, Act\n85 of 1993 and its Regulations, governing the duties and obligations of a Service Provider / Designer\nperforming duties in terms of an agreement with the client (SANRAL). These duties are fully described\nin the Occupational Health and Safety Act, Act 85 of 1993 and its Regulations and it is the duty of every\nService Provider / Designer to acquaint themselves therewith before commencing work.\ng) Words used herein in the singular shall be deemed to include the plural and male shall include female\nand vice versa, unless the context otherwise requires.\n1. PURPOSE\nThis document is compiled to ensure that the Professional Service Provider are aware of the Occupational\nHealth and Safety requirements when working on a SANRAL contract, as well as to make them aware of their\nlegal liabilities and responsibilities as per the Occupational Health & Safety Act, Act 85 of 1993, and its\nRegulations.\n1. DEFINITIONS AND ABBREVIATIONS\nAssessment – An opinion or a judgment about someone or something that has been thought about very\ncarefully.\nAt-risk behavior – Conduct that unnecessarily increases the likelihood of an injury or incident.\nAudit – A systematic and documented review of the effectiveness of implementation of processes,\nprogrammes and procedures, based on general process criteria.\nBaseline risk assessment: This is the initial assessment of risk in a workplace. It is a broad assessment and\nincludes all activities taking place on site but does not include risk control measures or safeguards.\nCIDB – Construction Industry Development Board\nClient – Any organization or person for whom construction work is performed. For the purpose of this\ndocument, the client is the South African National Roads Agency SOC Limited.\nCommunicate – The process of two-way dialogue which is understood by both parties.\nCompetence – A combination of attributes such as knowledge, training, experience and qualifications to\nassure successful performance.\nCompetent Person – Means a person who has in respect of the work or task to be performed the required\nknowledge, training and experience and, where applicable, qualifications, specific to that work or task:\nProvided that where appropriate qualifications and training are registered in terms of the provisions of the\nNational Qualification Framework Act, 2000 (Act No. 67 of 2000), those qualifications and that training must\nbe regarded as the required qualifications and training; and is familiar with the Act and with the applicable\nregulations made under the Act.\nC3-112\n\nConsequence – Outcome or impact of an event.\nContinual Improvement – A recurring process of enhancing performance to achieve consistent\nimprovements in overall performance.\nContractor – An employer as defined in section 1 of the OHS Act, who performs construction work.\nConstruction Work – According to the Construction Regulations, any work in connection with:\n- The construction, erection, alteration, renovation, repair, demolition or dismantling of or addition to a\nbuilding or any similar structure; or\n- The construction, erection, maintenance, demolition or dismantling of any bridge, dam, canal, road,\nrailway, runway, sewer or water reticulation system; or the moving of earth, clearing of land, the making\nof excavation, piling, or any similar civil engineering structure or type of work.\nCorrective Action – An action taken to eliminate the cause of a detected non-conformity or other undesirable\nsituation.\nConstruction Regulations (CR) – Construction Regulations, GNR. 84 of 2014\nContractor – An employer who performs construction work.\nCritical equipment – A piece of equipment or a structure whose failure to perform to design specification, has\nthe potential to result in a major accident event.\nDesign – in relation to any structure, includes drawings, calculations, design details and specifications.\nDesigner –\na) competent person who:\n- Prepares a design;\n- Checks and approves a design;\n- Arranges for a person at work under his or her control to prepare a design, including an employee\nof that person where he or she is the employer; or\n- Designs temporary work, including its components;\nb) an architect or engineer contributing to, or having overall responsibility for a design;\nc) a building services engineer designing details for fixed plant;\nd) a surveyor specifying articles or drawing up specifications;\ne) a contractor carrying out design work as part of a design and building project; or\nf) an interior designer, shop fitter or landscape architect.\nDMR – Driven Machinery Regulations, GNR. 295 of 26 February 1988\nDocuments – Structured units of recorded information and its supporting medium (paper or electronic). Most\nrecords are documents, but not all documents are records. A document becomes a record when it is part of a\nbusiness transaction, is kept as evidence of that transaction and is managed within a record-keeping system.\nEIR – Electrical Installation Regulations, GNR. 242 of 6 March 2009\nEmergency – An abnormal occurrence that pose a threat to the safety or health of employees, customers, or\nlocal communities, or which can cause damage to assets or the environment.\nEmployee – An individual who is employed by or works for an Employer and who receives or is entitled to\nreceive any remuneration or who works under the direction or supervision of an employer or any other person.\nEmployer – Any person who employs or provides work for any person and remunerates that person or\nexpressly or tacitly undertakes to remunerates him but excludes a labour broker as defined in section 1(1) of\nthe Labour Relations Act, 1956 (Act No. 28 of 1956). For the purpose of this document, the employer is the\nSouth African National Roads Agency SOC Limited.\nEMR – Electrical Machinery Regulations, GNR. 250 of 25 March 2011\nEnvironment – The surroundings or conditions in which a person, animal or plant lives or operates, including\nair, water, land, natural resources and habitats.\nExcavation work – The making of any man-made cavity, trench, pit or depression formed by cutting, digging\nor scooping.\nC3-113\n\nGAR – General Administrative Regulations, GNR. 929 of 25 June 2003\nGMR – General Machinery Regulations, GNR. 1521 of 5 August 1988\nGSR – General Safety Regulations, GNR. 1031 of 30 May 1986\nHarm – A significant and or long-lasting adverse effect on people, the environment or the community.\nHazard – A source, situation or act with a potential for harm in terms of human injury or ill health.\nHealth and Safety File – Means a file, or other record in permanent form, containing the information in writing\nas required by the Construction Regulations, GNR. 84 of 7 February 2014, Section 7(1)(b).\nHealth and Safety Plan – Means a project specific documented plan in accordance with the client’s health\nand safety specifications, as required by the Construction Regulations, GNR. 84 of 7 February 2014, Section\n7(1)(a).\nHealth and Safety Specification – Means a project specific document prepared by the client pertaining to all\nhealth and safety requirements related to construction work, as required by the Construction Regulations,\nGNR. 84 of 7 February 2014, Section 5(1)(b).\nHSE – Health, Safety and Environment. Commonly used in the format HSE.\nIncident – Work-related events (including accidents which give rise to injury, ill health, fatality or emergencies)\nthat have resulted in, or has the potential to result in adverse consequences to people, the environment,\nproperty, reputation or a combination of these.\nLikelihood – A description of probability or frequency, in relation to the chance that something will occur.\nLost Time Injury (LTI) – When a person is injured during the execution of his/her duties and as a result of the\ninjury is unable to perform his/her regular duties for one full shift or more on the day following the day on which\nthe injury has incurred, whether a scheduled work day or not(weekend).\nManagement System – Management processes and documentation that collectively provide a systematic\nframework for ensuring that tasks are performed safely, correctly, consistently and effectively to achieve a\nspecified outcome and to drive continual improvement in performance.\nMandatory – An agent, contractor or sub-contractor for work, but without derogating from his status in his own\nright as an employer or a user.\nMSDS – Material Safety Data Sheet\nNear Hit / Near Miss – Any occurrence or situation which had the potential for adverse consequences to\npeople, the environment, property, reputation or a combination of these.\nNon-conformance – Any deviation from work standards, practices, procedures, regulations that could either\ndirectly or indirectly lead to injury or illness, property damage, damage to the environment or a combination of\nthese.\nOHS Act – Occupational Health & Safety Act, 85 of 1993\nPolicy – Statement by an organization of its intentions and principles in relation to its overall performance\nwhich provides a framework for action and for the setting of its objectives and targets.\nPPE – Personal Protective Equipment\nPreventive Action – An action implemented to eliminate the cause of a potential non-conformity or other\nundesirable potential situation.\nPrincipal Contractor – An employer appointed by the client to perform construction work and who is in overall\ncontrol and management of a part of or the whole construction site.\nProcedure – A specific documented way to carry out an activity or a process.\nC3-114\n\nRecords – Recorded information, in any form that is kept as evidence. Records include monitoring results,\nevidence of training, audits, inspections and calibration reports.\nRisk Assessment – A process of evaluating the risk(s) arising from hazards taking into account the adequacy\nof any existing controls and deciding whether or not the risk(s) is acceptable.\nRisk Management – The ongoing treatment of risks through the application of management policies,\nprocesses, procedures and risk control measures.\nRisk – A combination of the likelihood of an occurrence of a hazardous event or exposure and the severity of\ninjury or ill health that can be caused by the event or exposure.\nRoot Cause – The cause of the incident that, when rectified, will prevent the recurrence of not just incidents\nwith those exact circumstances, but others with similar causes.\nSACPCMP – South African Council for Project and Construction Management Professions\nSANRAL - South African National Roads Agency SOC Limited\nSupplier – A person or company that supplies material or equipment to a contractor on a construction site but\ndoes not physically carry out construction work on the construction site.\nThe Act – The Occupational Health and Safety Act No. 85 of 1993\nThe Site – The area where work is carried out for SANRAL as defined on the front page of this document.\nWAH – Acronym for Working at Heights\n1. HEALTH AND SAFETY POLICY\nService Providers are expected to have their own written Health and Safety Policy. The policy should declare\ntheir attitude and approach to the health, safety and welfare of their employees and others. Provision must be\nmade to review the policy annually and the CEO or Managing Director must sign and date the policy to indicate\nhis commitment to ensuring the health and safety of his employees.\n1. ROLES & RESPONSIBILITIES\nEvery Service Provider is considered to be an employer in his own right and shall comply with all legal\nrequirements pertaining to an employer, which include the responsibility to provide as far as reasonably\npracticable a safe and healthy working environment for his employees, as per Section 8 of the OHS Act.\nIn conjunction with Section 8 of the OHS Act, all employees on the project are responsible for their own safety\nas well as the safety of persons who may be affected by their acts, as per Section 14 of the OHS Act. It is the\nresponsibility of each employee to ensure that he acts in a safe manner before, during and after work is carried\nout.\nThe Service Provider shall ensure that where required by the OHS Act and Regulations, competent employees\nare appointed in writing. These appointments must be project / contract specific and specific to the tasks that\nwill be performed. Every appointment must display the duties of the person appointed and training certificates\nfrom a registered training provider must be attached to such appointment (where applicable).\n1. HSE TRAINING AND COMPETENCE\nWhere appropriate qualifications and training are registered in terms of the provisions of the National\nQualifications Framework Act, 2000 (Act No. 67 of 2000), those qualifications and training must be regarded\nas the required qualifications and training and employees must have attended courses of the aforementioned\nnature to be considered competent in the task.\nAll employees that form part of the construction work must be trained and competent. Employees formally\nappointed to perform a certain duty must be in possession of a training certificate, received from a registered\nC3-115\n\ntraining provider. All Service Provider employees must as a minimum have received site specific safety\ninduction training as well as task specific risk assessment training from the Principal Contractor.\nTraining Needs – There shall be a system in place to determine the training requirements of each individual,\nbased on the tasks that the employee will perform as well as to ensure the health and safety of fellow\nemployees and the public. Special attention should be given to employees who are new hires, new to the task\nor have combined responsibilities.\nBasic Safe Work Training (Induction Training) – Every Service Provider shall ensure that his employees are\ninducted into his own company Health and Safety System and must ensure that his employees receive site\nspecific safety induction and task specific risk assessment training from the Principal Contractor. The Service\nProvider must have evidence that his employees have been trained on the relevant procedures prior to and\nduring the project duration.\nFormal Training – All qualifications for which there are SAQA registered training courses, must be regarded\nas the minimum required qualifications and training. To be deemed “competent” an employee must have\nreceived training at a registered training provider, the training course must be registered and if there is an\nassessment, the employee must have been found competent after the assessment. A person cannot be\ndeemed competent after awareness training only.\nThe Service Provider shall ensure that his employees, have received appropriate training for the type of work\nthat will be performed, e.g. Working at Heights, Risk Assessment training etc.\nRecords – Record of all training shall be kept by the Service Provider and shall be readily available. Records\nshall make provision for refresher training where applicable. Where an employee is legally appointed with\ncertain duties and responsibilities a copy of the training certificate must be attached to the appointment.\n1. NOTIFICATION OF CONSTRUCTION WORK\nConstruction Regulation, 2014 Section 4 requires that the provincial director of the Department of Labour is\nnotified at least 7 days prior to the execution of excavation work, elevated work where there is a risk of falling\n(working at heights), demolition work or work where explosives are used. Therefore, if the Service Provider\nneeds to e.g. dig test pits, or do a bridge inspection and the risk of falling exists, the Service Provider needs\nto notify the provincial director in writing on a form similar to Annexure 2 in the Construction Regulations\n1. DUTIES\nThe Service Provider is the Designer of the structures to be build. As a Designer, the Service Provider need\nto comply and adhere to the requirements of Section 6 of the Construction Regulations. The Service Provider\nneeds to take health and safety into consideration when designing the structure as well as for future\nmaintenance on the structure.\nWhen the Service Provider carry out any type of construction work, as per the definition, the Service Provider\nwill be regarded as a Contractor, as per the definition, and must then comply with the requirements of the\nConstruction Regulations and in particular Section 7.\nThe Service Provider is the Clients managing agent, who will manage the construction work on behalf of the\nclient.\n1. DESIGNING FOR HEALTH, SAFETY AND THE ENVIRONMENT\nDesigning for safety is a process aimed at minimizing injury, death, property damage or destruction and harm\nto the environment, by utilizing an approach to identify and eliminate or control hazardous areas. The Service\nProvider / Designer must implement a process that ensures safety is incorporated in the design process, which\nincludes temporary works as contemplated in the Construction Regulations, 2014 Section 12.\nThe Service Provider must communicate the anticipated risks and hazards resulting from the design to the\nClient and the Client Construction Health and Safety Agent who will ensure that such anticipated risks and\nhazards reflects in the tender documentation for Contractors.\nC3-116\n\n1. MANAGEMENT AND SUPERVISION\nThe Service Provider will manage the construction project on behalf of SANRAL and must ensure that the\nconstruction work is carried out safely and legal compliance is adhered to at all times. As the managing agent,\nthe Service Provider must appoint a competent person in writing as agent to act as the Clients representative\nin terms of health and safety on the project.\n1. RISK MANAGEMENT\nWhen the Service Provider carry out any type of construction work, Section 9 of the Construction Regulations\nmust be adhered to. A formal risk based approach must be followed to ensure hazard control measures are\nimplemented to an acceptable reasonable practical level. The Service Provider and his employees shall be\nresponsible to ensure all hazards pertaining to his scope of activity are proactively identified, the risks assessed\nand appropriately eliminated or minimized and managed on an ongoing basis. Risk assessments shall also\nidentify possible and potential environmental, health and hygiene issues pertaining to each hazard with\npotential exposures and limits.\na) Hazard Identification and Risk Assessment (Construction Regulation 9)\ni. Development of Risk Assessments\nThe Service Provider shall, before carrying out any type of construction work and during such\nwork, conduct a risk assessment by a competent person, appointed in writing. The baseline risk\nassessment as provided by the client may be used to draw up an in-depth task specific risk\nassessment that can be used on site. Please note that the risk assessment must be site specific.\nThe risk assessment shall include, as far as is reasonably practicable, at least:\n• The task and task step\n• the identification of the risks and hazards to which persons may be exposed to during the\ntask or task step;\n• the analysis and evaluation of the risks and hazards identified, inclusive of a residual risk\nrating methodology. The method to be used is not prescribed;\n• a documented plan of safe work procedures, to mitigate, reduce or control those residual\nrisks that have been identified as unacceptably high, by means of the rating system;\n• a monitoring plan;\n• a review plan, inclusive of dates to be adhered to; and\n• ergonomic related risks are to be analysed, evaluated and addressed as part of the\nprocess.\nb) Risk Assessment Monitoring\nThe Service Provider shall ensure that a monitoring plan for all risk assessments are in place. Risk\nassessments must be monitored to ensure effectiveness and employee understanding. The monitoring\nof risk assessments shall be formal, and records thereof shall be available for audit purposes.\nc) Review of Risk Assessment\nThe Service Provider shall review the hazard identification, risk assessments and standard safe working\nprocedures prior to any construction related work activity and shall ensure that the risk assessment is\nsite specific.\nActivities carried out without conducting a risk assessment or found to be non-compliant with the risk assess-\nment, will be stopped until such time a risk assessment is compiled, and work is carried out according to the\nrisk assessment.\nRisk assessments must be fully communicated to all relevant personnel and must be considered when\nestablishing training, awareness and competency requirements.\n11.1 Baseline Risk Assessment\nBased on the baseline risk assessment as per 20.1 below, SANRAL has developed this health and\nsafety specification which shall act as a set of OH&S rules that shall be applied to regulate the OH&S\naspects of the Service Provider’s construction work to be carried out. The baseline risk assessment\nC3-117\n\nmust be used by the Service Provider to develop task specific risk assessments before any construction\nrelated work commences.\nThe Baseline Risk Assessment will not identify risks or control measures, this must be identified by\nService Provider when preparing the Issue Based Risk Assessments. The Baseline Risk Assessment\nwill highlight all work for which the Service Provider must prepare safe work procedures and or work\nmethod statements.\n11.2 Continuous Risk Assessment\nThe Service Provider shall continuously assess the risks of the activities that are carried out. Risk\nassessments must be in writing, site specific and must be reviewed continuously to ensure it is current\nand it address all the relevant hazards and risks associated with the specific activity at the specific site.\nThe Risk assessment must be discussed with the whole work crew before the activity starts and the\nwork crew must acknowledge in writing having discussed the risk assessment and that they understand\nit. This acknowledgement must be on site and must be available to the client for audit purposes.\n1. LEGAL COMPLIANCE AND DOCUMENT CONTROL\nThe Service Provider is required to implement systems and procedures to ensure legal compliance through:\n- Identification of all relevant HSE legislation, standards and codes applicable to its operations.\n- Have available copies of all relevant HSE legislation, standards and codes for reference purposes.\n- Update legislation, standards and codes with any changes\n- Communicate to all employees any changes that may affect their accountabilities and conformances\n- Incorporate any legal requirements into their HSE management system and designs.\n- Monitor and review their HSE management system for effectiveness.\nThe Service Provider shall, as a minimum, comply with:\n- The Occupational Health and Safety Act and Regulations (Act 85 of 1993), an up-to-date copy of which\nshall be available on site at all times.\n- The Compensation for Occupational Injuries and Diseases Act (Act 130 of 1993), an up-to-date copy of\nwhich shall be available on site at all times.\n- Where work is being carried out on a “mine”, the Service Provider shall comply with the Mines Health\nand Safety Act and Regulations (Act 29 of 1960) and any other OH&S requirements that the mine may\nspecify. An up-to-date copy of the Mines Health and Safety Act and Regulations shall be available on\nsite at all times.\nWherever in the Construction Regulations or this specification there is reference to other regulations (e.g.\nConstruction Regulation 24: Electrical Installations and Machinery on Construction Sites) the Service Provider\nshall be conversant with and shall comply with these regulations.\n12.1 Legal Appointments\nAll legal appointments of the Service Provider regarding the Health and Safety of his employees who\nare to carry out construction work on the project are addressed and governed by the OHS Act and\napplicable Regulations.\ni. Overall Supervision and Responsibility for OH&S\nThe client will appoint the Service Provider who shall be the managing agent of the client on the\nproject once construction works start. A Mandatory agreement as per Section 37.2 of the OHS\nAct, shall be signed between the client and the Service Provider.\nIt is a requirement that the Service Provider, when he appoints sub Service Providers, includes a\nMandatary agreement in his agreement with such sub Service Providers\nii. Specific Supervision Responsibilities for OH&S\nThe Service Provider shall appoint designated competent employees and/or other competent\npersons as required by the OHS Act and Regulations, as well as this specification. Appointments\nshall be in writing and the responsibilities clearly stated together with the period for which the\nappointment is made. This information shall be communicated to and agreed with the appointees.\nWhere applicable, the training certificate must be attached to the appointment. Notice of\nC3-118\n\nappointments shall be submitted to the Employer. All changes shall also be communicated to the\nEmployer.\nBelow is a list of possible appointments for the project, which is not an all-inclusive list, but for reference\npurposes only:\nAppointment Legal Reference\nAssistant to CEO OHS Act 16(2)\nConstruction Health & Safety Agent CR 5(5)(6)\nRisk Assessor CR 9(1)\nFall Protection Plan Developer CR 10(1)(a)\nStructure Inspector CR 11(2)(a)\nTemporary Works Designer CR 12(1)\nExcavation Supervisor CR 13(1)(a)\nIncident investigator GAR 9(2)\nLadder inspector GSR 13(a)\nFirst Aider GSR GSR 3(4)\n1. OPERATIONAL INTEGRITY\nThe operational integrity of plant, equipment, structures and protective systems must be monitored and\nassured on an ongoing basis throughout the project cycle. Hazards must be identified, assessed and as far as\nreasonably practicable, eliminated or the risks treated to as low as reasonably practicable (ALARP).\n13.1 Plant & Equipment Integrity\n13.1.1 Construction Plant & Equipment\nThe Service Provider shall maintain all his items of plant and equipment necessary to perform the\nrequired construction work in a safe condition.\nThe client reserves the right to inspect items of plant and equipment brought to site and used on\nsite by the Service Provider. Should it be found that any item is inadequate, faulty, unsafe or in\nany other way unsuitable for the safe and satisfactory execution of the work for which it is\nintended, the Service Provider will be advised of such observation / inspection, and the Service\nProvider shall be required to repair, make safe or remove such item from operation and replace\nit with a safe and adequate substitute.\nThe Service Provider shall ensure that all plant, equipment, and power tools that he brings onto\nand use on site for construction work purposes are:\n- Appropriate for the type of work to be performed.\n- Placed on a register and inspected by a competent person or the authorized operator\nbefore use, daily or monthly dependent on Legislation and project requirements.\n- Record inspection findings on a register that must be kept on site.\n- The inspection register shall reflect the serial number of the plant, equipment or power tool.\n- Maintained and used in accordance with the manufacturer’s recommendations.\n- Have adequate machine guarding fitted to all exposed rotating or moving parts, as\nreasonably practicable, that have the potential to cause harm.\n- All electrical power supply units are protected with operational earth leakage devices.\n- Any defective, damaged or sub-standard equipment must be marked as unsafe for use and\nremoved from operation as soon as possible.\n13.1.2 Standards and Registers\nFor construction work purposes, the Service Provider is expected to:\n- Set up an initial set of registers as per the requirements of the OHS Act and Regulations.\n- Complete the registers for each piece of plant, tool and equipment brought on and used on\nsite by the Service Provider or his employees.\nC3-119\n\n- Maintain a complete, continuous and comprehensive inspection and service history in\nthese registers or checklists.\n- Ensure daily, weekly, monthly inspections are done and recorded for all plant, tools &\nequipment by a competent person as required by the OHS Act and Regulations.\n1. OCCUPATION HEALTH AND HYGIENE\n14.1 Medical Fitness for Duty\nAll Service Provider employees that carry out construction work activities, shall undergo medical\nexaminations and be certified fit for duty by an Occupational Health Practitioner before they are allowed\nto work on site.\nThe medical certificate must be in the form of Annexure 3 of the Construction Regulations and stipulate\nthe possible exposures the employee might be exposed to during the execution of the construction work.\nIt is recommended and in the best interest of the Service Provider to implement pre-employment as well\nas exit medical surveillance, especially with regards to Section 8 of the Noise Induced Hearing Loss\nRegulation.\n14.2 First Aid\nAccording to GSR 3(4), where more than 10 employees are employed at a workplace/worksite, the\nService Provider shall ensure that there is at least one trained first aider for every group of 50 employees\nat the workplace/site. First Aid boxes must be provided where more than 5 employees are employed\nand must be readily available and accessible for the treatment of injured persons at the workplace.\nTo ensure immediate treatment of an injured person, it is recommended that all work crews have at\nleast one trained first aider, with a fully stocked first aid box, irrespective of the number of people in the\nwork crew. This is especially important when Service Providers work at great distances from the nearest\nemergency facility or town. These persons shall be appointed in writing as the first aiders with their\ncertificates attached as proof of competency.\nThe minimum contents of the first aid box shall be as per the supplied list in the General Safety\nRegulations.\nAll treatments done must be recorded on a register and kept with the first aid box. A trained and\nappointed first aider must be responsible for the first aid box and its content. Used content must be\nreplenished as soon as possible.\nIn order to ensure prompt response at the emergency facility it is recommended that the W.Cl 2 forms\nbe partially completed with the employers’ details, for medical treatment cases.\n14.3 Workers Compensation Registration\nThe Service Provider shall ensure that his employees are covered for any occupational injuries and\nillnesses in terms of the Occupational Injuries and Diseases Act 130 of 1993, which cover shall remain\nin place and up to date for the duration of the project.\n14.4 Hygiene Facilities\nThe Service Provider shall ensure compliance to Section 30 of the Construction Regulations with\nregards to facilities on the construction site as well as where accommodation is provided to employees\non remote sites. The Service Provider shall ensure that the facilities are kept clean at all times, either\nthrough a Service Provider or self-employed persons.\n14.5 Health related Epidemics and Pandemics\nThe Service Provider shall, ensure that the requirements stipulated in the Hazardous Biological Agents\n(HBA) Regulation are adhered to and in particular the following as described in the mentioned\nRegulation:\n• Information and training of employees\n• Duties of person that may be exposed to HBA’s\nC3-120\n\n• Risk Assessments by the employer (Service Provider)\n• Monitoring exposure at the workplace\n• Medical surveillance of employees\n• Keeping of records\n• Control of Exposure to HBA’s\n• Personal Protective Equipment and facilities\n• Maintenance of control measures and facilities\n• Prohibitions\n1. WASTE MANAGEMENT\nThe Service Provider shall comply with all applicable and relevant Waste management legislation, as well as\nmunicipal bylaws applicable to waste management.\nIt is recommended that the Service Provider have a waste management plan which must be implemented on\nthe construction site and which will have the objective to ensure that waste is managed according to the Waste\nManagement Hierarchy:\n- Reduce what you can. If you cannot reduce then,\n- Re-use what you can. If you cannot re-use then,\n- Recycle what you can. What you cannot recycle,\n- Convert into energy sources. If it cannot be converted to an energy source,\n- Dispose of in a landfill – this is only to be done as a last resort and disposed without endangering human\nhealth and without using processes or methods which could harm the environment.\n1. HAZARDOUS SUBSTANCE MANAGEMENT\nThe Service Provider shall ensure that hazardous substances brought onto site are easily identifiable and\nstored according to the requirements of the General Safety Regulations, GNR. 1031 of 1986, Section 4.\nWhere flammable liquids are being used or stored, this must be done in a manner which would not cause a\nfire or explosion hazard.\nThe Service Provider shall have Material Safety Data Sheets (MSDS) readily available for flammable,\nhazardous and toxic chemical substances and materials brought onto site and shall ensure that his employees\nare trained in these MSDS’s.\nFlammable, hazardous or toxic chemical substances may not be stored in empty food or drink containers.\nEmpty flammable, hazardous and toxic containers must be disposed of in a safe manner, which will prevent\nfurther use of such a container.\nAsbestos\nA survey of the construction site must be done during site establishment, to locate any asbestos. Should\nasbestos be located, the conditions of the Asbestos Regulations, GNR. 155 of 2002 must be followed and\ncomplied with.\n1. OPERATIONAL PROCEDURES\nEach construction activity shall be assessed by the Service Provider so as to identify operational procedures\nthat will mitigate against the occurrence of an incident during the execution of each activity. This specification\nrequires the Service Provider:\n- to be conversant with all relevant Regulations;\n- to comply with their provisions;\n- to include them in his OH&S plan where relevant\n1. HSE NON-COMPLIANCE\nThe Client has a legal duty in terms of Construction Regulation 5(1)(q) to stop any unsafe work on the\nconstruction site. Any unsafe construction related activity that the Service Provider performs will be stopped\nuntil such time as the Service Provider has rectified the non-compliance or unsafe act/condition.\nC3-121\n\nThe Service Provider as managing agent for the Client has a legal duty according to the Construction\nRegulation 5(1)(q) to stop a contractor from executing any activity which poses a threat to the health and safety\nof persons. Depending on the seriousness of the non-compliance only the specific activity may be stopped\nuntil the non-compliance is rectified or the whole operation may be stopped.\nIt is also the duty of every employee to take reasonable care of his own health and safety and of other persons\nwho may be affected by his acts as per OHS Act, Section 14(a). Keeping this in mind, it is required of the\nService Provider to ensure his employees has the right to remove themselves from any unsafe situation or\nwork activity, without any negative consequence to them until such time as the Service Provider has made the\nunsafe situation or activity as safe as practicable possible.\n18.1 Contracting Philosophy\nAny site specific hazards and safety management expectations will be made known to the Service\nProvider prior to the work commencing on site. Legal OHS requirements contained in the OHS Act and\nRegulations as well as SANS Codes are the minimum requirements the Service Provider must apply\nduring this contract with regards to Occupational Health and Safety. The Service Provider shall apply,\nimplement and enforce the minimum OHS Act & Regulations and SANS Codes requirements.\n18.2 Indemnity by Service Provider\nThe Service Provider shall indemnify the Employer against and from all damages, losses and expenses\n(including legal fees and expenses) resulting from:\n(a) the loss of output and delay caused by the slowing down or partial or total stoppage of work\ncaused by:\ni. Unsafe acts caused by all or any of the Service Provider’s employees;\nii. Unsafe conditions which resulted from the failure to carry out any legal obligation by all or\nany of the Service Provider’s employees;\n(b) any unlawful, riotous or disorderly conduct by or amongst the Service Provider’s employees.\n18.3 Service Provider Conduct\nGuidelines to the most important rules that shall be implemented and maintained by the Service\nProvider:\n- Complete compliance to the OH&S Act and Regulations\n- Hazard identification and Risk Assessments for all construction related activities\n- DSTI talk before construction work commences\n- Safe access and egress to and from work areas.\n- Compulsory use of lifelines, Safety Harnesses and Fall Arrestors (Lanyards to be attached at all\ntimes) when working in elevated positions\n- Good housekeeping\n- Securing of tools, equipment and material at heights\n- Wearing of appropriate personal protective equipment as identified in the risk assessment\nPersonal Conduct\nNo person shall act in a manner that endangers or is likely to endanger, the safety of any other person,\nor cause harm to any other person.\nAn employee who observes any dangerous situation, shall as soon as possible inform the person who\nis responsible for that section of the site.\nAny employee who becomes aware of any person disregarding any safety rules, shall remind that\nperson of the rules. If he persists in disregarding the rules, the matter must be reported to his supervisor.\nNo person shall damage, alter, remove, render ineffective or interfere with anything that has been\nprovided for the protection of the site, or for the health and safety of persons.\nNo person shall interfere with or use firefighting equipment without authority and training.\nNo person in a state of intoxication or condition that render him incapable of controlling himself shall\nenter or be allowed to enter the site.\nC3-122\n\nNo alcohol or illegal drugs shall be taken onto the site.\nAll safety and warning signs shall be obeyed.\nAlways be alert of construction vehicles as well as traffic. Never turn your back to oncoming traffic or\nconstruction vehicles / mobile plant, always have a line of sight.\n18.4 Sub Service Providers\nThe Service Provider shall establish, maintain and ensure that all his Sub Service Providers establish\nand maintain HSE standards and systems as necessary and to comply with the Legal requirements as\nwell as these HSE specifications.\n18.5 Public Health and Safety\nThe Service Provider shall, as far as is reasonably practicable, be responsible for ensuring that non-\nemployees affected by the construction work are made aware of the dangers likely to arise from said\nconstruction work as well as the precautionary measures to be observed to avoid or minimise those\ndangers. During the construction phase of the project, this can be done through the Principal Contractors\nHSE Officer.\nThis includes:\n• Non- employees entering the site for whatever reason\n• The surrounding community\n• Passers-by to the site\n1. INCIDENT MANAGEMENT\nThe Service Provider shall ensure that a culture exists within his company that promotes the recognition,\nresponse, reporting and investigation of incidents, including near misses (near hits). The Service Provider must\nimplement a procedure for reporting and investigating accidents, incidents and near misses as prescribed in\nthe General Administrative Regulation, Section 8. The Service Provider should have a clear objective and\ntarget to obtain zero injuries for the duration of the project and such an objective must be communicated to all\nemployees.\nAppropriate corrective actions must be implemented and the applicable learnings must be shared within the\nService Provider’s business to prevent a recurrence of the incident or to prevent the minor incidents from\nbecoming serious incidents in future.\n19.1 Incidents and Accidents\nThe Service Provider shall investigate all accidents/incidents where employees were injured to the\nextent that he had to be referred for medical treatment by a doctor, hospital or clinic. The results of the\ninvestigation shall be entered into an accident/incident register, which must be updated with each\naccident/indent.\nThe Service Provider shall notify the relevant SANRAL Project Manager and or SANRAL OHS Specialist\nof any incident / accident within the Service Provider’s area of responsibility in writing as soon as\npossible.\nAlthough the accident / incident is reported to the client, the Service Provider has a responsibility and is\nrequired by law to report any Section 24 accidents and incidents to the Department of Labour. Any road\ntraffic accident must be reported to the relevant authorities.\nIt is essential that the Service Provider demonstrate that corrective and preventative action has been\ntaken to prevent a similar incident in future and that it is communicated to all affected employees. A\ncopy of the investigation, corrective and preventative action taken as well as the attendance register of\nthe employees who attended the discussion of the incident and the action implemented to prevent a\nsimilar incident, must be forwarded to the SANRAL Project Manager and or the SANRAL OHS\nSpecialist.\nIn addition to medical treatment cases, it is recommended that Near Miss incidents and First Aid cases\nare also investigated to establish root causes and implement preventative measures.\nC3-123\n\n1. PROJECT SPECIFIC CONSTRUCTION REQUIREMENTS\n20.1 Baseline Risk Assessment\nThe following is a list of risks identified which forms the Baseline Risk Assessment for the project\nprepared by the Client in terms of Construction Regulation 5(1)(a):\nRisks in connection with:\n• Working in elevated positions (e.g. Bridge Inspections)\n• Working over water environments (e.g. Bridge Inspections)\n• Excavation – locating existing underground services, digging test pits\n• Manual handling – setting up surveying equipment\n• Ergonomic risks\n• High & Low voltage power lines – overhead & underground\n• Work in close proximity to railway lines\n• Uneven ground surfaces\n• Personal safety & security risks\n• Traffic Control (Ensure Correct Signage at Correct Distances)\n• Working from ladders\n• Exposure to noise\n• Working in close proximity to traffic\n• Working with hot materials\n• Radio Active equipment\n• Hazardous Chemical Substances\n• Flammable Materials\n• Driving to and from site\n• Biological risks e.g. bees, snakes, spiders, etc.\n• Environmental risks\n- Bad weather conditions,\no rain,\no lightning,\no wind,\n- poor visibility\n- heat exposure – dehydration\n- cold environments\nDuring the construction phase of the project, the employees of the Service Provider must be inducted\nand trained on the risk assessments and Safe Operating Procedures of the Principal Contractor before\nentering the site and record of such induction / training must be kept on file, e.g.\n• Work in close proximity of Mobile Plant (e.g. Bomag Roller, Pneumatic roller, etc)\n• Falling Objects\n• Suspended Loads\n• Crane Operations\n• Asphalting\n20.2 Site Attendance Register\nAll site visitors shall report to security / reception upon arrival at site. All visitors need to sign an\nattendance register when visiting the site. Visitors include all persons which are not permanently working\non the site but excludes temporary site workers. Visitors must undergo site induction training before they\nare allowed on site to make them aware of the site dangers. During the construction stage of the project,\nthe HSE Officer of the Principal Contractor should conduct the site induction with visitors before they\nare allowed on the construction site.\n20.3 Personal Protective Equipment\nComply with General Safety Regulations, Section 2\nThe Service Provider shall identify the hazards in the workplace and remove them or, where\nimpracticable, take steps to protect employees and make it possible for them to work safely and without\nrisk to health under the hazardous conditions.\nC3-124\n\nPersonal protective equipment (PPE) should, however, be the last resort. The hierarchy of hazard\nelimination must be followed before the option of personal protective equipment is considered. The\nfollowing hierarchy of controls must be followed:\n- Elimination\n- Passive Controls\no Substitution – Using a cherry picker or man-lift instead of a ladder.\no Engineering Controls – Installing barrier railings; Installing stairs instead of using vertical\nladders.\n- Active Controls\no Administrative policies and procedures\no Personal protective equipment\nWhere it is not possible to create an absolutely safe and healthy workplace the Service Provider shall\ninform employees regarding this and issue, free of charge, suitable equipment to protect them from any\nhazards being present and that allows them to work safely and without risk to health in the hazardous\nenvironment.\nIt is a further requirement that the Service Provider maintain the said equipment, that he instructs and\ntrains the employees in the use of the equipment and ensures that the prescribed equipment is used by\nthe employee/s.\nEmployees do not have the right to refuse the use/wear of the equipment prescribed by the Employer\nand, if it is impossible for an employee to use or wear prescribed protective equipment through health\nor any other reason, the employee cannot be allowed to continue working under the hazardous\ncondition/s for which the equipment was prescribed but an alternative solution has to be found that may\ninclude relocating the employee.\nThe Service Provider shall ensure that all his personnel, excluding those who are permanently office\nbound, are equipped with reflective safety jackets and that these are worn at all times when working on\nsite. Any person found not wearing a reflective jacket on site must be removed from the site until such\ntime as he is in possession of and wearing a reflective jacket. Reflective safety jackets shall be kept in\ngood condition and any jackets that are ineffective must immediately be replaced by the Service\nProvider.\n20.4 Site Security\nCertain areas where work must be carried out, is recognized unsafe areas and certain other areas may\nfrom time to time become unsafe, due to 3rd party actions. The Service Provider must, as far as\nreasonably possible, anticipate unsafe areas and must ensure that his site staff is safe from 3rd party\nactions, which include but is not limited to:\n- Unrests,\n- Violent Demonstrations,\n- Theft,\n- Injury from 3rd parties at all times.\nThe Service Provider must, when work is to be carried out in the above-mentioned areas, make provision\nfor security services to accompany site staff during the execution of their work, as the Service Provider\nis responsible for the Health, Safety and Security of his own staff. The provision for security services\nmust form part of the Service Provider’s tender.\n20.5 Working in Elevated Positions\nComply with Construction Regulation, Section 10\nThe Service Provider shall ensure that a fall protection plan, developed by a competent person who is\ndesignated as the Fall Protection Plan Developer, is available on site and understood by all employees\nwho will be working in elevated positions.\nAll employees working in elevated positions shall protect themselves from falls by wearing a full body\nharness and the lanyard shall be attached as far as possible above the head of the employee to a life\nline or other approved and tested anchor point.\nIn addition to obvious elevated work activities, work activities which include:\nC3-125\n\n- Working on the edge of an excavation where there is a risk of falling into the excavation;\n- Work on the edge of a vertical drop where there is a risk of falling;\n- Work on top of trucks and tanks;\nShall be considered work in elevated positions and Section 10 of the Construction Regulations must be\nadhered to at all times. The hierarchy of controls must be implemented when such activities are carried\nout. As a minimum the employee must wear PPE, which shall include a full body harness attached to a\nrestraint.\nOnly suitable ladders that have been inspected and has been certified “safe for use” may be used for\nelevated work. The top two rungs on the ladder may not be used for elevation, as this will render the\nladder unstable. A second person should hold the ladder stable whenever working from it.\n20.6 Excavations\nComply with Construction Regulations, Section 13\nThe Service Provider shall ensure that all excavations are carried out under the supervision of a\ncompetent person who has been appointed in writing as Excavation Supervisor.\nThe Service Provider must evaluate the stability of the ground before excavation work begins as well as\nduring excavation work.\nExcavations must be barricaded to prevent unauthorized access.\nMaterial removed from excavations, as well as heavy machinery and construction vehicles, must not be\ncloser than 1 meter of the edge of the excavation, to prevent additional loads on the excavation edge,\nwhich could cause cave-ins, to prevent construction vehicles from falling into the excavation and to\nprevent the accumulation of carbon monoxide gas inside the excavation.\nPeople working in the excavation must be adequately protected from cave-ins, by means of protection\nsystems such as shoring and bracing and must have a safe means of access into the excavation and\negress from the excavation.\n20.7 Construction Vehicles\nComply with Construction Regulation, Section 23, National Road Traffic Act, 1996\nConstruction vehicle operators must have received training to operate the class of construction vehicle\nor mobile plant and must be in possession of an operator’s card as proof of competency. Construction\nvehicle operators must be authorised in writing and have a medical certificate of fitness issued by an\noccupational health practitioner to operate the construction vehicle and/or mobile plant.\n20.8 Electrical Equipment\nComply with Construction Regulations, Section 24\nThe Service Provider shall take adequate steps to ascertain the presence of and guard against danger\nto workers from electrical cables or apparatus which is under, over or on the site.\nThe exact location of underground electric power cables must be determined before any excavators are\nused for excavation purposes.\nThe location of overhead electrical cables must be assessed when working with equipment that has\nbooms that can extent. Injury may be possible from touching the electrical cables with the equipment\nboom, or from arching when the equipment boom comes too close to the electrical cable.\nElectrical machinery and extension cords must be in a serviceable condition and must be inspected\nbefore use on the construction site by the authorised operator and the inspection checklist must be kept\non the construction site.\n20.9 Temporary Storage of Flammable Liquids\nComply with Construction Regulation, Section 25 and General Safety Regulations, Section 4\nC3-126\n\nThe Service Provider must ensure storage areas of flammable liquids are well ventilated and “No\nSmoking” signs are placed at the entrances and ventilation ducts of the storage areas. Firefighting\nequipment must be available in suitable positions around the storage areas.\nThe Service Provider must ensure that good housekeeping is practiced in and around the flammable\nstorage areas.\n20.10 Water Environments\nComply with Construction Regulation, Section 26\nThe Service Provider must ensure that a lifejacket forms part of the employees PPE and is worn when\nthe employee is exposed to the risk of drowning, by falling into water.\nThe risk assessment must make provision for the rescuing of persons in danger of drowning and for\npreventing employees from falling into the water.\nWhen working over water environments, Section 10 of the Construction Regulations – Fall Protection\nwill also apply.\n20.11 Manual Handling / Ergonomic Risks\nThe Service Provider must ensure that the health of his employees is not affected through the handling\nof heavy equipment and safe lifting techniques are applied by his employees. Manual handling and\nergonomic risks must be included in the risk assessments of the Service Provider and employees must\nbe aware of it and trained in it.\n20.12 Traffic Control\nWhen the Service Provider carries out construction related activities on site, during the design stage of\nthe project, sufficient and adequate traffic control must be implemented. Traffic control signage must be\ndisplayed, and employees must be aware of approaching traffic, facing oncoming traffic at all times.\n20.13 Radioactive Equipment\nThe Service Provider, when working with radioactive equipment, must ensure that all appropriate safety\nmeasures are implemented, employees are made aware of the dangers of the equipment and the\nequipment is used according to the manufacturer’s instructions.\n20.14 Intoxicating Liquor and Drugs\nComply with General Safety Regulations, Section 2A.\nThe site limit for intoxication is set to zero to complement a vision of zero tolerance.\nAny person found to be intoxicated, or consuming intoxicating liquor or illegal drugs, must not be allowed\nonto the premises and/or must be removed from the premises.\nThe Service Provider has the right to test any person entering the premises for intoxicating liquor or\nillegal drugs and may refuse entrance on the basis of the outcome of the test.\nThe Service Provider shall ensure that employees taking prescription medicine informs the Service\nProvider of such and shall ensure that the side effect of such medicine does not constitute a hazard to\nthe employee himself or people working in close proximity to the employee.\nC3-127\n\nD2: Health and Safety Specification for Contractors during Construction\nTHE SOUTH AFRICAN NATIONAL\nROADS AGENCY SOC LIMITED\nOCCUPATIONAL HEALTH AND SAFETY SPECIFICATIONS\nCONTRACT SANRAL C.002-056-2024/1\nSCOPE OF WORKS: COMMUNITY DEVELOPMENT (CD) PROJECT\nFOR R573-010-2023/1 UPGRADE OF KLOPPERSBOS/PYRAMID\nROAD FROM GRAVEL TO SURFACED.\nC3-128\n\nTABLE OF CONTENTS PAGE\n1. INCIDENT MANAGEMENT ............................................................................................ NOTE TO PRINCIPAL CONTRACTORS AND CONTRACTORS\nThe Occupational Health and Safety Act, Act 85 of 1993 and its Regulations together with SANS Codes set\nout minimum standards with regards to Occupational Health and Safety. The South African National Roads\nAgency SOC Limited (SANRAL), has developed this Occupational Health and Safety Specifications with these\nminimum standards in mind and in certain stages the requirements of SANRAL exceeds the minimum legal\nrequirements to follow best practices and to ensure a healthy and safe workplace for all.\nSANRAL in no way assumes the Contractors legal liabilities and responsibilities. The Contractor is and remains\naccountable for the quality and execution of his health and safety programme for his employees. This Health\nand Safety Specification reflects minimum legal and SANRAL requirements and should not be construed as\nall encompassing.\nIt is realized that the Contractor have its own Health and Safety Management system and safe work practices.\nThe intention of this Health and Safety Specification is not to change the Contractors Health and Safety\nmanagement system, but for the Contractor to use its current Health and Safety management system to draw\nup a project specific Health and Safety plan according to these specifications as well as to legally comply with\nthe Construction Regulations, GNR.84 of 2014.\nIt is the responsibility of the Principal Contractor and other Contractors to make themselves conversant and\ncomply with the requirements and conditions contained in the various legislation pertaining to their profession\nand scope of works at all times.\nThis document is not exhaustive of all duties imposed by the Occupational Health and Safety Act, Act 85 of\n1993 and its Regulations, governing the duties and obligations, of a Designer, Principal Contractor and\nContractor performing duties in terms of an agreement with the client (SANRAL). These duties are fully\ndescribed in the Occupational Health and Safety Act, Act 85 of 1993 and its Regulations and it is the duty of\nevery Designer, Principal Contractor and Contractor to acquaint themselves therewith before commencing\nwork.\nWords used herein in the singular shall be deemed to include the plural and male shall include female and\nvice versa unless the context otherwise requires.\n1. PURPOSE\nThis document is compiled to ensure that the Principal Contractor and any other Contractors working for\nSANRAL directly or through a Principal Contractor, are aware of the Occupational Health and Safety require-\nments when working on a SANRAL contract, as well as to make them aware of their legal liabilities and\nresponsibilities as per the Occupational Health & Safety Act, Act 85 of 1993, and its Regulations.\n1. DEFINITIONS AND ABBREVIATIONS\nAssessment – An opinion or a judgment about someone or something that has been thought about very\ncarefully.\nAt-risk behavior – Conduct that unnecessarily increases the likelihood of an injury or incident.\nAudit – A systematic and documented review of the effectiveness of implementation of processes,\nprogrammes and procedures, based on general process criteria.\nBaseline risk assessment: This is the initial assessment of risk in a workplace. It is a broad assessment and\nincludes all activities taking place on site but does not include risk control measures or safeguards.\nCIDB – Construction Industry Development Board\nClient – Any organization or person for whom construction work is performed. For the purpose of this\ndocument, the client is the South African National Roads Agency SOC Limited.\nCommunicate – The process of two-way dialogue which is understood by both parties.\nCompetence – A combination of attributes such as knowledge, training, experience and qualifications to\nassure successful performance.\nCompetent Person – Means a person who has in respect of the work or task to be performed the required\nknowledge, training and experience and, where applicable, qualifications, specific to that work or task:\nProvided that where appropriate qualifications and training are registered in terms of the provisions of the\nNational Qualification Framework Act, 2000 (Act No. 67 of 2000), those qualifications and that training must\nbe regarded as the required qualifications and training; and is familiar with the Act and with the applicable\nregulations made under the Act.\nC3-131\n\nConsequence – Outcome or impact of an event.\nContinual Improvement – A recurring process of enhancing performance to achieve consistent improvements\nin overall performance.\nContractor – An employer as defined in section 1 of the OHS Act, who performs construction work and\nincludes Principal Contractors and Sub-Contractors.\nConstruction Work – any work in connection with:\n- The construction, erection, alteration, renovation, repair, demolition or dismantling of or addition to a\nbuilding or any similar structure; or\n- The construction, erection, maintenance, demolition or dismantling of any bridge, dam, canal, road,\nrailway, runway, sewer or water reticulation system; or the moving of earth, clearing of land, the making\nof excavation, piling, or any similar civil engineering structure or type of work.\nCorrective Action – An action taken to eliminate the cause of a detected non-conformity or other undesirable\nsituation.\nConstruction Regulations (CR) – Construction Regulations, GNR. 84 of 2014\nCritical equipment – A piece of equipment or a structure whose failure to perform to design specification, has\nthe potential to result in a major accident event.\nDesign – in relation to any structure, includes drawings, calculations, design details and specifications.\nDesigner –\na) competent person who:\n- Prepares a design;\n- Checks and approves a design;\n- Arranges for a person at work under his or her control to prepare a design, including an employee\nof that person where he or she is the employer; or\n- Designs temporary work, including its components;\nb) an architect or engineer contributing to, or having overall responsibility for a design;\nc) a building services engineer designing details for fixed plant;\nd) a surveyor specifying articles or drawing up specifications;\ne) a contractor carrying out design work as part of a design and building project; or\nf) an interior designer, shop fitter or landscape architect\nDMR – Driven Machinery Regulations, GNR. 295 of 26 February 1988\nDocuments – Structured units of recorded information and its supporting medium (paper or electronic). Most\nrecords are documents, but not all documents are records. A document becomes a record when it is part of a\nbusiness transaction, is kept as evidence of that transaction and is managed within a record-keeping system.\nEIR – Electrical Installation Regulations, GNR. 242 of 6 March 2009\nEmergency – An abnormal occurrence that pose a threat to the safety or health of employees, customers, or\nlocal communities, or which can cause damage to assets or the environment.\nEmployee – An individual who is employed by or works for an Employer and who receives or is entitled to\nreceive any remuneration or who works under the direction or supervision of an employer or any other person.\nEmployer – Any person who employs or provides work for any person and remunerates that person or\nexpressly or tacitly undertakes to remunerates him but excludes a labour broker as defined in section 1(1) of\nthe Labour Relations Act, 1956 (Act No. 28 of 1956). For the purpose of this document, the employer is the\nSouth African National Roads Agency SOC Limited.\nEMR – Electrical Machinery Regulations, GNR. 250 of 25 March 2011.\nEnvironment – The surroundings or conditions in which a person, animal or plant lives or operates, including\nair, water, land, natural resources and habitats.\nExcavation work – The making of any man-made cavity, trench, pit or depression formed by cutting, digging\nor scooping.\nGAR – General Administrative Regulations, GNR. 929 of 25 June 2003\nC3-132\n\nGMR – General Machinery Regulations, GNR. 1521 of 5 August 1988\nGSR – General Safety Regulations, GNR. 1031 of 30 May 1986\nHarm – A significant and or long-lasting adverse effect on people, the environment or the community.\nHazard – A source, situation or act with a potential for harm in terms of human injury or ill health.\nHealth and Safety File – Means a file, or other record in permanent form, containing the information in writing\nas required by the Construction Regulations, GNR. 84 of 7 February 2014, Section 7(1)(b).\nHealth and Safety Plan – Means a project specific documented plan in accordance with the client’s health\nand safety specifications, as required by the Construction Regulations, GNR. 84 of 7 February 2014, Section\n7(1)(a).\nHealth and Safety Specification – Means a project specific document prepared by the client pertaining to all\nhealth and safety requirements related to construction work, as required by the Construction Regulations,\nGNR. 84 of 7 February 2014, Section 5(1)(b).\nHSE – Health, Safety and Environment. Commonly used in the format HSE.\nIncident – Work-related events (including accidents which give rise to injury, ill health, fatality or emergencies)\nthat have resulted in, or has the potential to result in adverse consequences to people, the environment,\nproperty, reputation or a combination of these.\nLikelihood – A description of probability or frequency, in relation to the chance that something will occur.\nLost Time Injury (LTI) – When a person is injured during the execution of his/her duties and as a result of the\ninjury is unable to perform his/her regular duties for one full shift or more on the day following the day on which\nthe injury has incurred, whether a scheduled work day or not(weekend).\nManagement System – Management processes and documentation that collectively provide a systematic\nframework for ensuring that tasks are performed safely, correctly, consistently and effectively to achieve a\nspecified outcome and to drive continual improvement in performance.\nMandatory – An agent, contractor or sub-contractor for work, but without derogating from his status in his own\nright as an employer or a user.\nMSDS – Material Safety Data Sheet\nNear Hit / Near Miss – Any occurrence or situation which had the potential for adverse consequences to\npeople, the environment, property, reputation or a combination of these.\nNon-conformance – Any deviation from work standards, practices, procedures, regulations that could either\ndirectly or indirectly lead to injury or illness, property damage, damage to the environment or a combination of\nthese.\nOHS Act – Occupational Health & Safety Act, 85 of 1993\nPolicy – Statement by an organization of its intentions and principles in relation to its overall performance\nwhich provides a framework for action and for the setting of its objectives and targets.\nPPE – Personal Protective Equipment\nPreventive Action – An action implemented to eliminate the cause of a potential non-conformity or other\nundesirable potential situation.\nPrincipal Contractor – An employer appointed by the client to perform construction work and who is in overall\ncontrol and management of a part of or the whole construction site.\nProcedure – A specific documented way to carry out an activity or a process.\nRecords – Recorded information, in any form that is kept as evidence. Records include monitoring results,\nevidence of training, audits, inspections and calibration reports.\nC3-133\n\nRisk Assessment – A process of evaluating the risk(s) arising from hazards taking into account the adequacy\nof any existing controls and deciding whether or not the risk(s) is acceptable.\nRisk Management – The ongoing treatment of risks through the application of management policies,\nprocesses, procedures and risk control measures.\nRisk – A combination of the likelihood of an occurrence of a hazardous event or exposure and the severity of\ninjury or ill health that can be caused by the event or exposure.\nRoot Cause – The cause of the incident that, when rectified, will prevent the recurrence of not just incidents\nwith those exact circumstances, but others with similar causes.\nSACPCMP – South African Council for Project and Construction Management Professions\nSANRAL - South African National Roads Agency SOC Limited\nSupplier – A person or company that supplies material or equipment to a contractor on a construction site but\ndoes not physically carry out construction work on the construction site.\nThe Act – The Occupational Health and Safety Act No. 85 of 1993\nThe Site – The area where work is carried out for SANRAL as defined on the front page of this document.\nWAH – Acronym for Working at Heights\n1. HEALTH AND SAFETY POLICY\nContractors are expected to have their own written Health and Safety Policy. The policy should declare their\nattitude and approach to the health, safety and welfare of their employees and others. Provision must be made\nto review the policy annually and the CEO or Managing Director must sign and date the policy to indicate his\ncommitment to ensuring the health and safety of his employees.\n1. ROLES & RESPONSIBILITIES\nEvery Contractor is considered to be an employer in his own right and shall comply with all legal requirements\npertaining to an employer, which include the responsibility to provide as far as reasonably practicable a safe\nand healthy working environment for his employees, as per Section 8 of the OHS Act.\nIn conjunction with Section 8 of the OHS Act, all employees on the project are responsible for their own safety\nas well as the safety of persons who may be affected by their acts, as per Section 14 of the OHS Act. It is the\nresponsibility of each employee to ensure that he acts in a safe manner before, during and after work is carried\nout.\nThe contractor shall ensure that where required by the OHS Act and Regulations, competent employees are\nappointed in writing. These appointments must be project / contract specific and specific to the tasks that will\nbe performed. Every appointment must display the duties of the person appointed and training certificates from\na registered training provider must be attached to such appointment (where applicable). A list of possible\nappointments can be found in section 11.1 below.\n1. HSE TRAINING AND COMPETENCE\nWhere appropriate qualifications and training are registered in terms of the provisions of the National\nQualifications Framework Act, 2000 (Act No. 67 of 2000), those qualifications and training must be regarded\nas the required qualifications and training and employees must have attended courses of the aforementioned\nnature to be considered competent in the task.\nAll employees that form part of the construction work must be trained and competent. Employees formally\nappointed to perform a certain duty must be in possession of a training certificate, received from a registered\ntraining provider. All employees must as a minimum have received site specific safety induction training and\nmust receive daily safe task instruction training (DSTI) before any work commences.\nC3-134\n\nTraining Needs – There shall be a system in place to determine the training requirements of each individual,\nbased on the tasks that the employee will perform as well as to ensure the health and safety of fellow\nemployees and the public. Special attention should be given to employees who are new hires, new to the task\nor have combined responsibilities.\nBasic Safe Work Training (Induction Training) – Every contractor shall ensure that his employees are inducted\ninto his own company Health and Safety System as well as basic safe work training (HSE Induction Training).\nThe Principal Contractor shall ensure that his, all his Sub-Contractor employees and visitors are inducted on\nthe specific site safety procedures.\nA Daily Safe Task Instruction (DSTI) must be conducted on site with all employees involved in the project. The\nDSTI must be carried out each day before work commences and proof thereof must be available on site. Each\nwork crew may conduct their own specific DSTI to discuss the hazards, risks and control measures associated\nwith their task for the day.\nWhere two or more contractors or work crews work in the same area, they should have a combined DSTI to\nensure they know of the additional hazards the other contractor or work crew will introduce to their operations\nand what precautions to put in place.\nThe contractor shall have evidence that employees have been trained on the relevant procedures prior to and\nduring the project duration.\nFormal Training – All qualifications for which there are SAQA registered training courses, must be regarded\nas the minimum required qualifications and training. To be deemed “competent” an employee must have\nreceived training at a registered training provider, the training course must be registered and if there is an\nassessment, the employee must have been found competent after the assessment. A person cannot be\ndeemed competent after awareness training only.\nThe Contractor shall ensure that his employees, as well as the employees of any sub-contractors that may be\nused, have received appropriate training for the type of work that will be performed, e.g. First Aid, Flag Man,\nMobile Plant Operator, Working at Heights, Risk Assessment training etc.\nRecords – Record of all training shall be kept by the employer and shall be readily available. Records shall\nmake provision for refresher training where applicable. Where an employee is legally appointed with certain\nduties and responsibilities a copy of the training certificate must be attached to the appointment.\n1. APPLICATION FOR CONSTRUCTION WORK PERMIT\nConstruction Regulation, 2014 Section 3 requires that the client apply for a construction work permit at least\n30 days before construction work is started, if the intended construction work is of a value exceeding forty\nmillion rand or CIDB grading level 8 and will start on or after 7 February 2017. If approved, the provincial\ndirector will issue a construction work permit in writing to perform construction work within 30 days of receiving\nthe application and assign a site-specific number for the construction site. It is the intention of SANRAL to\napply for a construction work permit as soon as the Contractor is appointed and his Health and Safety Plan is\nreceived, in order to minimize construction delays.\nThe site-specific construction work permit number must be displayed at the main entrance to the site and a\ncopy of the construction work permit must be kept in the principal contractor’s health and safety file for\ninspection purposes.\n1. DUTIES\nVarious duties are imposed on the client, designer, principal contractor and other contractors by the Con-\nstruction Regulation, 2014, Sections 5, 6 & 7. SANRAL will comply and carry out the required duties as con-\ntemplated in section 5 of the Construction Regulations, 2014 and it is expected from the designer and every\ncontractor to make themselves conversant with the requirements and duties imposed on them and to ensure\nthat they comply with the requirements of Section 6 and 7 at all times.\n1. MANAGEMENT AND SUPERVISION\nThe contractor shall ensure that the project is managed safely, and legal compliance is ensured at all times.\nC3-135\n\nA full-time competent person must be appointed as a Construction Manager to manage all construction work,\nincluding health and safety compliance. The construction manager may not be appointed to manage more\nthan one single construction site.\nThe construction manager must appoint construction supervisors responsible for construction activities and\nensuring occupation health and safety on the construction site.\nThe contractor must appoint a full-time or part-time construction health and safety officer, who is registered\nwith the SACPCMP, to assist in the control of health and safety aspects on site.\n1. RISK MANAGEMENT\nThe Contractor must follow a formal risk-based approach to ensure hazard control measures are implemented\nto an acceptable reasonable practical level. The contractor and his employees shall be responsible to ensure\nall hazards pertaining to his scope of activity are proactively identified, the risks assessed and appropriately\neliminated or minimized and managed on an ongoing basis. Risk assessments shall also identify possible and\npotential environmental, health and hygiene issues pertaining to each hazard with potential exposures and\nlimits.\na) Hazard Identification and Risk Assessment (Construction Regulation 9)\ni. Development of Risk Assessments\nThe contractor shall, before the commencement of any construction work or work associated with\nthe aforesaid construction work and during such work, conduct a risk assessment by a competent\nperson, appointed in writing and the risk assessment so produced shall form part of the OH&S\nplan and be implemented and maintained as contemplated in Construction Regulation 9(1).\nCompetence is a factor of training, knowledge, experience and/or appropriate qualifications.\nThe risk assessment shall include, as far as is reasonably practicable, at least:\n• The task or task step\n• the identification of the risks and hazards to which persons may be exposed during the task\nor task step;\n• the analysis and evaluation of the risks and hazards identified, inclusive of a residual risk\nrating methodology. The method to be used is not prescribed;\n• a documented plan of safe work procedures, to mitigate, reduce or control those residual\nrisks that have been identified as unacceptably high, by means of the rating system;\n• a monitoring plan;\n• a review plan, inclusive of dates to be adhered to; and\n• ergonomic related risks are to be analysed, evaluated and addressed as part of the\nprocess.\nBased on the risk assessments, the contractor shall develop a set of site-specific OH&S rules that\nshall be applied to regulate the OH&S aspects of the construction. The risk assessments, together\nwith the site-specific OH&S rules shall be submitted to the Employer before construction on site\ncommences. SANRAL has conducted a Baseline Risk Assessment as per 11.1 below, which must\nbe used by the contractor to develop task specific risk assessments before work commences.\nThis does not mean that all possible Risk Assessments must be attended to before work\ncommences, but that all relevant Risk Assessments receive the necessary attention as the\ncontract progresses, and this is the responsibility of the contractor.\nAll variations to the scope of work shall similarly be subjected to a risk assessment process.\nb) Risk Assessment Monitoring\nThe contractor shall ensure that a monitoring plan for all risk assessments are in place. Risk assess-\nments must be monitored to ensure effectiveness and employee understanding.
